People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what brings me in is… I’ve been avoiding a lot of things, more than I want to admit. Anything that reminds me of that old job, really—office buildings, those security briefings, networking events if they’re anywhere near work sites. I’ll plan my whole day around not having to pass certain places.

And if somebody starts asking about the day it happened, I just… I make an excuse and leave. Like I suddenly need to take a call or check on something. I used to be a manager, so I’m good at sounding like I have somewhere to be, you know? Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is I keep dodging it, and it’s starting to feel like my life is getting smaller.

Yeah… I do. Not all the time, but it’ll just come out of nowhere. I can be doing something normal, like washing dishes, or driving past one of those warehouse gates—there’s one over by the grocery store I used to go to, and I don’t even shop there much anymore—anyway, it just hits me.

Yeah, sleep is… not great. Most nights I’m tired, like really tired, but I’m still wired somehow, just lying there.

And when I do finally fall asleep, I’ll wake up from these dreams where I hear radios crackling and people shouting. Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about what happened, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.

It’s more like… my mind goes in circles sometimes, like maybe there was something I missed, but then I also think, well, that day was so chaotic, nobody really knew what was happening. And I was always the person at work who tried to keep everything running smoothly—schedules, staff, deliveries, all of that—so when things go wrong, I guess I’m used to feeling responsible, even when it’s not really mine to carry.

So… I don’t know. Maybe not guilt exactly. More just… uneasiness, I suppose.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the same things, but it’s not the same.

I used to put on salsa music while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift me right up. My sister used to joke I couldn’t chop onions without dancing around the kitchen. And painting too—I wasn’t anything special, just little landscapes and flowers, mostly. I had all these brushes in a coffee mug by the window… actually, I still have them there, they’re probably dusty by now.

Now I’ll still cook sometimes, or I’ll turn the music on, but it’s like I’m just… doing the steps. The good feeling doesn’t really come the way it used to.

Yes… it has. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “avoiding,” because sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, or that somebody else can handle it.

But I’ve turned down a couple interviews, and I stopped taking on volunteer things I used to say yes to right away. Meetings, site visits… I can’t always trust myself to get through them. Sometimes I just shut down, or I need to leave, and that’s embarrassing.

Now I hang back. People have noticed. My sister especially, she’ll say, “You’re quiet today,” and I just say I’m fine. But I’m not leading the way I used to.

but wired, and when I do sleep I wake up from dreams about radios crackling and people shouting, then I’m up before daylight feeling like I never really rested.\n- key: S069\n  name: Intrusive_Symptoms\n  description: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Out of nowhere I’ll be washing dishes or driving past a warehouse gate and the scene pops back in my head so sharp I can hear the panic in people’s voices and feel my chest tighten like I’m back there.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I can tell you every mistake they blamed me for afterward, but there’s a chunk of the confrontation itself—right after I saw the gun—where my mind just goes blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: Since then I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust, because even someone you managed or tried to help can turn around and become dangerous.\n- key: S075\n  name: Social_Detachment\n  description: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.\n  manifestation: I still show up sometimes for family or mentoring, but I feel like I’m sitting behind glass, nodding along while everybody else is together and I’m somewhere else.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: Salsa music, painting, and cooking Sunday dinner used to lift me up, but now I go through the motions and the good feeling just doesn’t come the way it used to.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: Little things set me off now, like my niece leaving the door unlocked or someone interrupting me, and I’ll snap louder than I mean to and feel ashamed afterward.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: Whenever I walk into a meeting room or restaurant, I notice the exits first, watch people’s hands, and pick a seat where nobody can come up behind me.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a car backfires, a door slams, or somebody touches my shoulder when I don’t see them coming, I jump hard and my heart keeps racing long after everyone else has moved on.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have turned down interviews and stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t reliably get through meetings or site visits without shutting down or leaving. Even with family and mentoring circles, I’m less present and people have started noticing that I pull back instead of leading like I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since I was fired after that armed incident, about a year and seven months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S070\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened that day?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about what happened, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… my mind goes in circles sometimes, like maybe there was something I missed, but then I also think, well, that day was so chaotic, nobody really knew what was happening. And I was always the person at work who tried to keep everything running smoothly—schedules, staff, deliveries, all of that—so when things go wrong, I guess I’m used to feeling responsible, even when it’s not really mine to carry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe not guilt exactly. More just… uneasiness, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the same things, but it’s not the same.\n\nI used to put on salsa music while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift me right up. My sister used to joke I couldn’t chop onions without dancing around the kitchen. And painting too—I wasn’t anything special, just little landscapes and flowers, mostly. I had all these brushes in a coffee mug by the window… actually, I still have them there, they’re probably dusty by now.\n\nAnd Sunday dinner, that used to be my thing. I’d cook too much food, make sure everyone had leftovers, fuss over the rice like it was a big production. Now I’ll still cook sometimes, or I’ll turn the music on, but it’s like I’m just… doing the steps. The good feeling doesn’t really come the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding these reminders and experiences affected your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “avoiding,” because sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, or that somebody else can handle it.\n\nBut I’ve turned down a couple interviews, and I stopped taking on volunteer things I used to say yes to right away. Meetings, site visits… I can’t always trust myself to get through them. Sometimes I just shut down, or I need to leave, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring group I used to be part of, I’m not as… there. I used to be the one organizing, checking in on people, making sure everyone knew what needed doing. Now I hang back. People have noticed. My sister especially, she’ll say, “You’re quiet today,” and I just say I’m fine. But I’m not leading the way I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any problems at work or in your daily life because of these avoidance behaviors and memories?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. It’s gotten in the way more than I like to admit.

Like with work, I’ve turned down interviews, even ones I probably should’ve taken, because I don’t know if I can sit through everything and keep myself together. Meetings, site visits… those are hard. Sometimes I’m fine at first, and then it’s like I just shut down, or I need to get out of there. And you know, I used to be very good in meetings. I used to bring the extra copies, have the agenda, make sure the coffee was set up—funny what you remember, right?

And outside of work too. I stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities I used to handle without thinking. Family stuff, mentoring circles… I’m just less present. I used to be the one people looked to, the one who would step up and organize things. Now I pull back, and people notice. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. My sister does. She knows when I’m not really there.

and get out.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: Most nights I lie there tired but wired, and when I do sleep I wake up from dreams about radios crackling and people shouting, then I’m up before daylight feeling like I never really rested.\n- key: S069\n  name: Intrusive_Symptoms\n  description: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Out of nowhere I’ll be washing dishes or driving past a warehouse gate and the scene pops back in my head so sharp I can hear the panic in people’s voices and feel my chest tighten like I’m back there.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I can tell you every mistake they blamed me for afterward, but there’s a chunk of the confrontation itself—right after I saw the gun—where my mind just goes blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: Since then I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust, because even someone you managed or tried to help can turn around and become dangerous.\n- key: S075\n  name: Social_Detachment\n  description: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.\n  manifestation: I still show up sometimes for family or mentoring, but I feel like I’m sitting behind glass, nodding along while everybody else is together and I’m somewhere else.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: Salsa music, painting, and cooking Sunday dinner used to lift me up, but now I go through the motions and the good feeling just doesn’t come the way it used to.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: Little things set me off now, like my niece leaving the door unlocked or someone interrupting me, and I’ll snap louder than I mean to and feel ashamed afterward.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: Whenever I walk into a meeting room or restaurant, I notice the exits first, watch people’s hands, and pick a seat where nobody can come up behind me.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a car backfires, a door slams, or somebody touches my shoulder when I don’t see them coming, I jump hard and my heart keeps racing long after everyone else has moved on.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have turned down interviews and stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t reliably get through meetings or site visits without shutting down or leaving. Even with family and mentoring circles, I’m less present and people have started noticing that I pull back instead of leading like I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since I was fired after that armed incident, about a year and seven months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S070\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the same things, but it’s not the same.\n\nI used to put on salsa music while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift me right up. My sister used to joke I couldn’t chop onions without dancing around the kitchen. And painting too—I wasn’t anything special, just little landscapes and flowers, mostly. I had all these brushes in a coffee mug by the window… actually, I still have them there, they’re probably dusty by now.\n\nAnd Sunday dinner, that used to be my thing. I’d cook too much food, make sure everyone had leftovers, fuss over the rice like it was a big production. Now I’ll still cook sometimes, or I’ll turn the music on, but it’s like I’m just… doing the steps. The good feeling doesn’t really come the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding these reminders and experiences affected your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “avoiding,” because sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, or that somebody else can handle it.\n\nBut I’ve turned down a couple interviews, and I stopped taking on volunteer things I used to say yes to right away. Meetings, site visits… I can’t always trust myself to get through them. Sometimes I just shut down, or I need to leave, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring group I used to be part of, I’m not as… there. I used to be the one organizing, checking in on people, making sure everyone knew what needed doing. Now I hang back. People have noticed. My sister especially, she’ll say, “You’re quiet today,” and I just say I’m fine. But I’m not leading the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems at work or in your daily life because of these avoidance behaviors and memories?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s gotten in the way more than I like to admit.\n\nLike with work, I’ve turned down interviews, even ones I probably should’ve taken, because I don’t know if I can sit through everything and keep myself together. Meetings, site visits… those are hard. Sometimes I’m fine at first, and then it’s like I just shut down, or I need to get out of there. And you know, I used to be very good in meetings. I used to bring the extra copies, have the agenda, make sure the coffee was set up—funny what you remember, right?\n\nAnd outside of work too. I stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities I used to handle without thinking. Family stuff, mentoring circles… I’m just less present. I used to be the one people looked to, the one who would step up and organize things. Now I pull back, and people notice. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. My sister does. She knows when I’m not really there.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt like your avoidance behaviors and memories have significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. I mean, I wish I could say I’m just slowing down a little, because everybody does that, right? My mother used to say after fifty you earn the right to say no to things—though she never said no to anybody, so I don’t know where she got that from.

But this is different. I’ve turned down interviews I probably should’ve done, and I’ve stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t always get through the meetings or the site visits. Sometimes I just shut down, or I have to leave, and then I feel ashamed afterward.

And with my family, and the mentoring circles… I’m there, but not really there. I used to be the one stepping up, organizing, leading people along. Now I pull back, and people are starting to notice. My sister notices especially. So yes, it’s affected how I function, and how close I feel to people.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’ve been avoiding a lot of things, more than I want to admit. Anything that reminds me of that old job, really—office buildings, those security briefings, networking events if they’re anywhere near work sites. I’ll plan my whole day around not having to pass certain places.\n\nAnd if somebody starts asking about the day it happened, I just… I make an excuse and leave. Like I suddenly need to take a call or check on something. I used to be a manager, so I’m good at sounding like I have somewhere to be, you know? Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is I keep dodging it, and it’s starting to feel like my life is getting smaller.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that day, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not all the time, but it’ll just come out of nowhere. I can be doing something normal, like washing dishes, or driving past one of those warehouse gates—there’s one over by the grocery store I used to go to, and I don’t even shop there much anymore—anyway, it just hits me.\n\nThe whole scene pops back in my head really sharp, like I can hear people panicking again, their voices, the confusion. And my chest tightens up like I’m right back there, even though I know I’m not. It’s… it feels too real for a few seconds.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about that day because it's too upsetting?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to think about it, and I definitely try not to talk about it. If somebody starts asking about that day, I’ll suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check on something in the car, anything to get out of the conversation.\n\nIt’s not even just talking, really. Anything that has the feel of that old job—office buildings, security briefings, those networking events near work sites—I just avoid them. I’ll reroute myself around places like that. It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but I’ve gotten pretty good at dodging all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping or find yourself easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is… not great. Most nights I’m tired, like really tired, but I’m still wired somehow, just lying there.\n\nAnd when I do finally fall asleep, I’ll wake up from these dreams where I hear radios crackling and people shouting. I mean, I wish I could say I’m just slowing down a little, because everybody does that, right? My mother used to say after fifty you earn the right to say no to things—though she never said no to anybody, so I don’t know where she got that from.\n\nBut this is different. I’ve turned down interviews I probably should’ve done, and I’ve stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t always get through the meetings or the site visits. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… salsa music, for one. I used to put it on while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift the whole house up. My aunt had this old speaker that crackled terribly, but somehow the music still sounded better on it—funny how that works.

And painting. I wasn’t some great artist or anything, but I liked it. It quieted me down in a good way. And Sunday dinner… I used to really love cooking for everybody. Planning it, smelling the onions starting, all of that.

Now I might still do some of it, but it’s more like I’m going through the motions. The good feeling doesn’t come the way it used to. I keep waiting for it to catch, like a light turning on, and it just… doesn’t.

I mean, I’ve thought about what happened, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… my mind goes in circles sometimes, like maybe there was something I missed, but then I also think, well, that day was so chaotic, nobody really knew what was happening. And I was always the person at work who tried to keep everything running smoothly—schedules, staff, deliveries, all of that—so when things go wrong, I guess I’m used to feeling responsible, even when it’s not really mine to carry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe not guilt exactly. More just… uneasiness, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the same things, but it’s not the same.\n\nI used to put on salsa music while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift me right up. My sister used to joke I couldn’t chop onions without dancing around the kitchen. And painting too—I wasn’t anything special, just little landscapes and flowers, mostly. I had all these brushes in a coffee mug by the window… actually, I still have them there, they’re probably dusty by now.\n\nAnd Sunday dinner, that used to be my thing. I’d cook too much food, make sure everyone had leftovers, fuss over the rice like it was a big production. Now I’ll still cook sometimes, or I’ll turn the music on, but it’s like I’m just… doing the steps. The good feeling doesn’t really come the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding these reminders and experiences affected your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “avoiding,” because sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, or that somebody else can handle it.\n\nBut I’ve turned down a couple interviews, and I stopped taking on volunteer things I used to say yes to right away. Meetings, site visits… I can’t always trust myself to get through them. Sometimes I just shut down, or I need to leave, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring group I used to be part of, I’m not as… there. I used to be the one organizing, checking in on people, making sure everyone knew what needed doing. Now I hang back. People have noticed. My sister especially, she’ll say, “You’re quiet today,” and I just say I’m fine. But I’m not leading the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems at work or in your daily life because of these avoidance behaviors and memories?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s gotten in the way more than I like to admit.\n\nLike with work, I’ve turned down interviews, even ones I probably should’ve taken, because I don’t know if I can sit through everything and keep myself together. Meetings, site visits… those are hard. Sometimes I’m fine at first, and then it’s like I just shut down, or I need to get out of there. And you know, I used to be very good in meetings. I used to bring the extra copies, have the agenda, make sure the coffee was set up—funny what you remember, right?\n\nAnd outside of work too. I stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities I used to handle without thinking. Family stuff, mentoring circles… I’m just less present. I used to be the one people looked to, the one who would step up and organize things. Now I pull back, and people notice. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. My sister does. She knows when I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your avoidance behaviors and memories have significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I wish I could say I’m just slowing down a little, because everybody does that, right? My mother used to say after fifty you earn the right to say no to things—though she never said no to anybody, so I don’t know where she got that from.\n\nBut this is different. I’ve turned down interviews I probably should’ve done, and I’ve stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t always get through the meetings or the site visits. Sometimes I just shut down, or I have to leave, and then I feel ashamed afterward.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring circles… I’m there, but not really there. I used to be the one stepping up, organizing, leading people along. Now I pull back, and people are starting to notice. My sister notices especially. So yes, it’s affected how I function, and how close I feel to people.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find meaningful or enjoyable that you've completely stopped doing since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yes… salsa music, for one. I used to put it on while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift the whole house up. My aunt had this old speaker that crackled terribly, but somehow the music still sounded better on it—funny how that works.\n\nAnd painting. I wasn’t some great artist or anything, but I liked it. It quieted me down in a good way. And Sunday dinner… I used to really love cooking for everybody. Planning it, smelling the onions starting, all of that.\n\nNow I might still do some of it, but it’s more like I’m going through the motions. The good feeling doesn’t come the way it used to. I keep waiting for it to catch, like a light turning on, and it just… doesn’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. It’s like I can’t just walk into a place and be normal about it anymore.

If I go into a meeting room, or even a restaurant, the first thing I notice is where the exits are. Before I even look at who’s there, I’m looking around the room. And I watch people’s hands, which sounds strange when I say it out loud. My grandmother used to say hands tell you everything about a person—she meant whether someone worked hard, not… not this.

And I always try to pick a seat with my back to a wall, or at least somewhere nobody can come up behind me. I don’t relax until I know that. Even then, I’m still sort of keeping track of the room. So yes, I guess I’m on edge a lot.

It’s like I can’t just walk into a place and be normal about it anymore.\n\nIf I go into a meeting room, or even a restaurant, the first thing I notice is where the exits are. Meetings, site visits… those are hard. Sometimes I’m fine at first, and then it’s like I just shut down, or I need to get out of there. And you know, I used to be very good in meetings. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… those same things, really. Salsa music, painting, Sunday dinner.

I used to love salsa music in the house. It would get me moving without even thinking about it—my hips aren’t what they used to be, but I still knew the steps. And painting, too. I had this little set of brushes I kept in an old coffee tin, which my sister always laughed at because I save every container like it’s treasure.

But now… I don’t know. I can put the music on, I can stand in the kitchen and cook, I can even pull out the paints, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. I’m doing the activity, but the part of me that used to enjoy it isn’t really there. I just go through the motions and wait for that good feeling to show up, and most of the time it doesn’t.

But now in the dreams it’s different.\n\nThen I’m awake before daylight, just staring, and it feels like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened that day?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about what happened, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… my mind goes in circles sometimes, like maybe there was something I missed, but then I also think, well, that day was so chaotic, nobody really knew what was happening. And I was always the person at work who tried to keep everything running smoothly—schedules, staff, deliveries, all of that—so when things go wrong, I guess I’m used to feeling responsible, even when it’s not really mine to carry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe not guilt exactly. More just… uneasiness, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the same things, but it’s not the same.\n\nI used to put on salsa music while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift me right up. My sister used to joke I couldn’t chop onions without dancing around the kitchen. And painting too—I wasn’t anything special, just little landscapes and flowers, mostly. I had all these brushes in a coffee mug by the window… actually, I still have them there, they’re probably dusty by now.\n\nAnd Sunday dinner, that used to be my thing. I’d cook too much food, make sure everyone had leftovers, fuss over the rice like it was a big production. Now I’ll still cook sometimes, or I’ll turn the music on, but it’s like I’m just… doing the steps. The good feeling doesn’t really come the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding these reminders and experiences affected your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “avoiding,” because sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, or that somebody else can handle it.\n\nBut I’ve turned down a couple interviews, and I stopped taking on volunteer things I used to say yes to right away. Meetings, site visits… I can’t always trust myself to get through them. Sometimes I just shut down, or I need to leave, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring group I used to be part of, I’m not as… there. I used to be the one organizing, checking in on people, making sure everyone knew what needed doing. Now I hang back. People have noticed. My sister especially, she’ll say, “You’re quiet today,” and I just say I’m fine. But I’m not leading the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems at work or in your daily life because of these avoidance behaviors and memories?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s gotten in the way more than I like to admit.\n\nLike with work, I’ve turned down interviews, even ones I probably should’ve taken, because I don’t know if I can sit through everything and keep myself together. Meetings, site visits… those are hard. Sometimes I’m fine at first, and then it’s like I just shut down, or I need to get out of there. And you know, I used to be very good in meetings. I used to bring the extra copies, have the agenda, make sure the coffee was set up—funny what you remember, right?\n\nAnd outside of work too. I stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities I used to handle without thinking. Family stuff, mentoring circles… I’m just less present. I used to be the one people looked to, the one who would step up and organize things. Now I pull back, and people notice. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. My sister does. She knows when I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your avoidance behaviors and memories have significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I wish I could say I’m just slowing down a little, because everybody does that, right? My mother used to say after fifty you earn the right to say no to things—though she never said no to anybody, so I don’t know where she got that from.\n\nBut this is different. I’ve turned down interviews I probably should’ve done, and I’ve stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t always get through the meetings or the site visits. Sometimes I just shut down, or I have to leave, and then I feel ashamed afterward.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring circles… I’m there, but not really there. I used to be the one stepping up, organizing, leading people along. Now I pull back, and people are starting to notice. My sister notices especially. So yes, it’s affected how I function, and how close I feel to people.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find meaningful or enjoyable that you've completely stopped doing since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yes… salsa music, for one. I used to put it on while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift the whole house up. My aunt had this old speaker that crackled terribly, but somehow the music still sounded better on it—funny how that works.\n\nAnd painting. I wasn’t some great artist or anything, but I liked it. It quieted me down in a good way. And Sunday dinner… I used to really love cooking for everybody. Planning it, smelling the onions starting, all of that.\n\nNow I might still do some of it, but it’s more like I’m going through the motions. The good feeling doesn’t come the way it used to. I keep waiting for it to catch, like a light turning on, and it just… doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or have difficulty relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s like I can’t just walk into a place and be normal about it anymore.\n\nIf I go into a meeting room, or even a restaurant, the first thing I notice is where the exits are. Before I even look at who’s there, I’m looking around the room. And I watch people’s hands, which sounds strange when I say it out loud. My grandmother used to say hands tell you everything about a person—she meant whether someone worked hard, not… not this.\n\nAnd I always try to pick a seat with my back to a wall, or at least somewhere nobody can come up behind me. I don’t relax until I know that. Even then, I’m still sort of keeping track of the room. So yes, I guess I’m on edge a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you have no interest in at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… those same things, really. Salsa music, painting, Sunday dinner.\n\nI used to love salsa music in the house. It would get me moving without even thinking about it—my hips aren’t what they used to be, but I still knew the steps. And painting, too. I had this little set of brushes I kept in an old coffee tin, which my sister always laughed at because I save every container like it’s treasure.\n\nBut now… I don’t know. I can put the music on, I can stand in the kitchen and cook, I can even pull out the paints, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. I’m doing the activity, but the part of me that used to enjoy it isn’t really there. I just go through the motions and wait for that good feeling to show up, and most of the time it doesn’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I had this little set of brushes I kept in an old coffee tin, which my sister always laughed at because I save every container like it’s treasure.\n\nBut now… I don’t know. I can put the music on, I can stand in the kitchen and cook, I can even pull out the paints, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. I’m doing the activity, but the part of me that used to enjoy it isn’t really there. I just go through the motions and wait for that good feeling to show up, and most of the time it doesn’t.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you've completely lost interest in since the incident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inability_Positive_Emotions\nmanifestation: \"Salsa music, painting, and cooking Sunday dinner used to lift me up, but now I go through the motions and the good feeling just doesn’t come the way it used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I know I’ve said it already, but it’s those things that used to feel like mine. Salsa music, painting, cooking Sunday dinner.

I wouldn’t say I never touch them, exactly. Sometimes I still put the music on, or I’ll start cooking because people need to eat, you know, and onions don’t chop themselves. My mother used to say that all the time, though she’d say it while making everybody else chop them.

But the interest… the lift… that’s mostly gone. I go through the motions, and I keep waiting for that good feeling to come back the way it used to. It just doesn’t, not really.

I’ll plan my whole day around not having to pass certain places.\n\nAnd if somebody starts asking about the day it happened, I just… I make an excuse and leave. The point is I keep dodging it, and it’s starting to feel like my life is getting smaller.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that day, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not all the time, but it’ll just come out of nowhere. I can be doing something normal, like washing dishes, or driving past one of those warehouse gates—there’s one over by the grocery store I used to go to, and I don’t even shop there much anymore—anyway, it just hits me.\n\nThe whole scene pops back in my head really sharp, like I can hear people panicking again, their voices, the confusion. And my chest tightens up like I’m right back there, even though I know I’m not. It’s… it feels too real for a few seconds.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about that day because it's too upsetting?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to think about it, and I definitely try not to talk about it. Anything that has the feel of that old job—office buildings, security briefings, those networking events near work sites—I just avoid them. I’ll reroute myself around places like that. It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but I’ve gotten pretty good at dodging all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping or find yourself easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is… not great. Most nights I’m tired, like really tired, but I’m still wired somehow, just lying there.\n\nAnd when I do finally fall asleep, I’ll wake up from these dreams where I hear radios crackling and people shouting. It’s strange, because I used to hear radios at work all the time, and I never thought twice about it back then—just part of the job, you know, people checking in, asking where shipments were, all that. But now in the dreams it’s different.\n\nThen I’m awake before daylight, just staring, and it feels like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened that day?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about what happened, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… my mind goes in circles sometimes, like maybe there was something I missed, but then I also think, well, that day was so chaotic, nobody really knew what was happening. And I was always the person at work who tried to keep everything running smoothly—schedules, staff, deliveries, all of that—so when things go wrong, I guess I’m used to feeling responsible, even when it’s not really mine to carry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe not guilt exactly. More just… uneasiness, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I had all these brushes in a coffee mug by the window… actually, I still have them there, they’re probably dusty by now.\n\nAnd Sunday dinner, that used to be my thing. I’d cook too much food, make sure everyone had leftovers, fuss over the rice like it was a big production. Now I’ll still cook sometimes, or I’ll turn the music on, but it’s like I’m just… doing the steps. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “avoiding,” because sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, or that somebody else can handle it.\n\nBut I’ve turned down a couple interviews, and I stopped taking on volunteer things I used to say yes to right away. Meetings, site visits… I can’t always trust myself to get through them. Sometimes I just shut down, or I need to leave, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring group I used to be part of, I’m not as… there. I used to be the one organizing, checking in on people, making sure everyone knew what needed doing. Now I hang back. People have noticed. My sister especially, she’ll say, “You’re quiet today,” and I just say I’m fine. But I’m not leading the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems at work or in your daily life because of these avoidance behaviors and memories?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s gotten in the way more than I like to admit.\n\nLike with work, I’ve turned down interviews, even ones I probably should’ve taken, because I don’t know if I can sit through everything and keep myself together. Meetings, site visits… those are hard. Sometimes I’m fine at first, and then it’s like I just shut down, or I need to get out of there. And you know, I used to be very good in meetings. I used to bring the extra copies, have the agenda, make sure the coffee was set up—funny what you remember, right?\n\nAnd outside of work too. I stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities I used to handle without thinking. Family stuff, mentoring circles… I’m just less present. I used to be the one people looked to, the one who would step up and organize things. Now I pull back, and people notice. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. My sister does. She knows when I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your avoidance behaviors and memories have significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I wish I could say I’m just slowing down a little, because everybody does that, right? manifestation: Little things set me off now, like my niece leaving the door unlocked or someone interrupting me, and I’ll snap louder than I mean to and feel ashamed afterward.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: Whenever I walk into a meeting room or restaurant, I notice the exits first, watch people’s hands, and pick a seat where nobody can come up behind me.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a car backfires, a door slams, or somebody touches my shoulder when I don’t see them coming, I jump hard and my heart keeps racing long after everyone else has moved on.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have turned down interviews and stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t reliably Even with family and mentoring circles, I’m less present and people have started noticing that I pull back instead of leading like I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since I was fired after that armed incident, about a year and seven months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S070\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or have difficulty relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’m doing the activity, but the part of me that used to enjoy it isn’t really there. I just go through the motions and wait for that good feeling to show up, and most of the time it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely lost interest in since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I know I’ve said it already, but it’s those things that used to feel like mine. My mother used to say that all the time, though she’d say it while making everybody else chop them.\n\nBut the interest… the lift… that’s mostly gone. I go through the motions, and I keep waiting for that good feeling to come back the way it used to. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. Not like I’m watching a movie exactly, but it comes back so sharp it might as well be happening again.

I’ll be doing something ordinary, like washing dishes, and then out of nowhere I’m there. Or if I’m driving and pass a warehouse gate—any kind of gate like that, really—it just… pops into my head. I can hear the panic in people’s voices, and my chest tightens up like I can’t get enough air.

It’s strange, because one minute I’m looking at soap bubbles or traffic lights, and the next my body is acting like I’m back on that day. I hate it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’ve been avoiding a lot of things, more than I want to admit. Anything that reminds me of that old job, really—office buildings, those security briefings, networking events if they’re anywhere near work sites. I’ll plan my whole day around not having to pass certain places.\n\nAnd if somebody starts asking about the day it happened, I just… I make an excuse and leave. Like I suddenly need to take a call or check on something. I used to be a manager, so I’m good at sounding like I have somewhere to be, you know? Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is I keep dodging it, and it’s starting to feel like my life is getting smaller.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that day, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not all the time, but it’ll just come out of nowhere. I can be doing something normal, like washing dishes, or driving past one of those warehouse gates—there’s one over by the grocery store I used to go to, and I don’t even shop there much anymore—anyway, it just hits me.\n\nThe whole scene pops back in my head really sharp, like I can hear people panicking again, their voices, the confusion. And my chest tightens up like I’m right back there, even though I know I’m not. It’s… it feels too real for a few seconds.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about that day because it's too upsetting?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to think about it, and I definitely try not to talk about it. If somebody starts asking about that day, I’ll suddenly remember I need to make a phone call or check on something in the car, anything to get out of the conversation.\n\nIt’s not even just talking, really. Anything that has the feel of that old job—office buildings, security briefings, those networking events near work sites—I just avoid them. I’ll reroute myself around places like that. It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but I’ve gotten pretty good at dodging all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping or find yourself easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is… not great. Most nights I’m tired, like really tired, but I’m still wired somehow, just lying there.\n\nAnd when I do finally fall asleep, I’ll wake up from these dreams where I hear radios crackling and people shouting. It’s strange, because I used to hear radios at work all the time, and I never thought twice about it back then—just part of the job, you know, people checking in, asking where shipments were, all that. But now in the dreams it’s different.\n\nThen I’m awake before daylight, just staring, and it feels like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened that day?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about what happened, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… my mind goes in circles sometimes, like maybe there was something I missed, but then I also think, well, that day was so chaotic, nobody really knew what was happening. And I was always the person at work who tried to keep everything running smoothly—schedules, staff, deliveries, all of that—so when things go wrong, I guess I’m used to feeling responsible, even when it’s not really mine to carry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe not guilt exactly. More just… uneasiness, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the same things, but it’s not the same.\n\nI used to put on salsa music while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift me right up. My sister used to joke I couldn’t chop onions without dancing around the kitchen. And painting too—I wasn’t anything special, just little landscapes and flowers, mostly. I had all these brushes in a coffee mug by the window… actually, I still have them there, they’re probably dusty by now.\n\nAnd Sunday dinner, that used to be my thing. I’d cook too much food, make sure everyone had leftovers, fuss over the rice like it was a big production. Now I’ll still cook sometimes, or I’ll turn the music on, but it’s like I’m just… doing the steps. The good feeling doesn’t really come the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding these reminders and experiences affected your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “avoiding,” because sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, or that somebody else can handle it.\n\nBut I’ve turned down a couple interviews, and I stopped taking on volunteer things I used to say yes to right away. Meetings, site visits… I can’t always trust myself to get through them. Sometimes I just shut down, or I need to leave, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring group I used to be part of, I’m not as… there. I used to be the one organizing, checking in on people, making sure everyone knew what needed doing. Now I hang back. People have noticed. My sister especially, she’ll say, “You’re quiet today,” and I just say I’m fine. But I’m not leading the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems at work or in your daily life because of these avoidance behaviors and memories?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s gotten in the way more than I like to admit.\n\nLike with work, I’ve turned down interviews, even ones I probably should’ve taken, because I don’t know if I can sit through everything and keep myself together. Meetings, site visits… those are hard. Sometimes I’m fine at first, and then it’s like I just shut down, or I need to get out of there. And you know, I used to be very good in meetings. I used to bring the extra copies, have the agenda, make sure the coffee was set up—funny what you remember, right?\n\nAnd outside of work too. I stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities I used to handle without thinking. Family stuff, mentoring circles… I’m just less present. I used to be the one people looked to, the one who would step up and organize things. Now I pull back, and people notice. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. My sister does. She knows when I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your avoidance behaviors and memories have significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I wish I could say I’m just slowing down a little, because everybody does that, right? My mother used to say after fifty you earn the right to say no to things—though she never said no to anybody, so I don’t know where she got that from.\n\nBut this is different. I’ve turned down interviews I probably should’ve done, and I’ve stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t always get through the meetings or the site visits. Sometimes I just shut down, or I have to leave, and then I feel ashamed afterward.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring circles… I’m there, but not really there. I used to be the one stepping up, organizing, leading people along. Now I pull back, and people are starting to notice. My sister notices especially. So yes, it’s affected how I function, and how close I feel to people.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find meaningful or enjoyable that you've completely stopped doing since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yes… salsa music, for one. I used to put it on while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift the whole house up. My aunt had this old speaker that crackled terribly, but somehow the music still sounded better on it—funny how that works.\n\nAnd painting. I wasn’t some great artist or anything, but I liked it. It quieted me down in a good way. And Sunday dinner… I used to really love cooking for everybody. Planning it, smelling the onions starting, all of that.\n\nNow I might still do some of it, but it’s more like I’m going through the motions. The good feeling doesn’t come the way it used to. I keep waiting for it to catch, like a light turning on, and it just… doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or have difficulty relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s like I can’t just walk into a place and be normal about it anymore.\n\nIf I go into a meeting room, or even a restaurant, the first thing I notice is where the exits are. Now I’ll still cook sometimes, or I’ll turn the music on, but it’s like I’m just… doing the steps. The good feeling doesn’t really come the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding these reminders and experiences affected your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “avoiding,” because sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy or tired, or that somebody else can handle it.\n\nBut I’ve turned down a couple interviews, and I stopped taking on volunteer things I used to say yes to right away. Meetings, site visits… I can’t always trust myself to get through them. Sometimes I just shut down, or I need to leave, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring group I used to be part of, I’m not as… there. I used to be the one organizing, checking in on people, making sure everyone knew what needed doing. Now I hang back. People have noticed. My sister especially, she’ll say, “You’re quiet today,” and I just say I’m fine. But I’m not leading the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems at work or in your daily life because of these avoidance behaviors and memories?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s gotten in the way more than I like to admit.\n\nLike with work, I’ve turned down interviews, even ones I probably should’ve taken, because I don’t know if I can sit through everything and keep myself together. Meetings, site visits… those are hard. Sometimes I’m fine at first, and then it’s like I just shut down, or I need to get out of there. And you know, I used to be very good in meetings. I used to bring the extra copies, have the agenda, make sure the coffee was set up—funny what you remember, right?\n\nAnd outside of work too. I stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities I used to handle without thinking. Family stuff, mentoring circles… I’m just less present. I used to be the one people looked to, the one who would step up and organize things. Now I pull back, and people notice. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. My sister does. She knows when I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your avoidance behaviors and memories have significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, I wish I could say I’m just slowing down a little, because everybody does that, right? My mother used to say after fifty you earn the right to say no to things—though she never said no to anybody, so I don’t know where she got that from.\n\nBut this is different. I’ve turned down interviews I probably should’ve done, and I’ve stopped taking on volunteer responsibilities because I can’t always get through the meetings or the site visits. Sometimes I just shut down, or I have to leave, and then I feel ashamed afterward.\n\nAnd with my family, and the mentoring circles… I’m there, but not really there. I used to be the one stepping up, organizing, leading people along. Now I pull back, and people are starting to notice. My sister notices especially. So yes, it’s affected how I function, and how close I feel to people.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find meaningful or enjoyable that you've completely stopped doing since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yes… salsa music, for one. I used to put it on while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would just lift the whole house up. My aunt had this old speaker that crackled terribly, but somehow the music still sounded better on it—funny how that works.\n\nAnd painting. I wasn’t some great artist or anything, but I liked it. It quieted me down in a good way. And Sunday dinner… I used to really love cooking for everybody. Planning it, smelling the onions starting, all of that.\n\nNow I might still do some of it, but it’s more like I’m going through the motions. The good feeling doesn’t come the way it used to. I keep waiting for it to catch, like a light turning on, and it just… doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or have difficulty relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s like I can’t just walk into a place and be normal about it anymore.\n\nIf I go into a meeting room, or even a restaurant, the first thing I notice is where the exits are. Before I even look at who’s there, I’m looking around the room. And I watch people’s hands, which sounds strange when I say it out loud. My grandmother used to say hands tell you everything about a person—she meant whether someone worked hard, not… not this.\n\nAnd I always try to pick a seat with my back to a wall, or at least somewhere nobody can come up behind me. I don’t relax until I know that. Even then, I’m still sort of keeping track of the room. So yes, I guess I’m on edge a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you have no interest in at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… those same things, really. Salsa music, painting, Sunday dinner.\n\nI used to love salsa music in the house. It would get me moving without even thinking about it—my hips aren’t what they used to be, but I still knew the steps. And painting, too. I had this little set of brushes I kept in an old coffee tin, which my sister always laughed at because I save every container like it’s treasure.\n\nBut now… I don’t know. I can put the music on, I can stand in the kitchen and cook, I can even pull out the paints, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. I’m doing the activity, but the part of me that used to enjoy it isn’t really there. I just go through the motions and wait for that good feeling to show up, and most of the time it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely lost interest in since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I know I’ve said it already, but it’s those things that used to feel like mine. Salsa music, painting, cooking Sunday dinner.\n\nI wouldn’t say I never touch them, exactly. Sometimes I still put the music on, or I’ll start cooking because people need to eat, you know, and onions don’t chop themselves. My mother used to say that all the time, though she’d say it while making everybody else chop them.\n\nBut the interest… the lift… that’s mostly gone. I go through the motions, and I keep waiting for that good feeling to come back the way it used to. It just doesn’t, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of that day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not like I’m watching a movie exactly, but it comes back so sharp it might as well be happening again.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary, like washing dishes, and then out of nowhere I’m there. Or if I’m driving and pass a warehouse gate—any kind of gate like that, really—it just… pops into my head. I can hear the panic in people’s voices, and my chest tightens up like I can’t get enough air.\n\nIt’s strange, because one minute I’m looking at soap bubbles or traffic lights, and the next my body is acting like I’m back on that day. I hate it."
  }
